## FRAGMENT PENETRATION 

#### 5 -49PENETRATION OF FRAGMENTS INTO STEEL.5-49.1Failure Mechanisms

In deriving a prediction equation for the penetration and perforation of steel plates, it is important to recognize the failure mechanisms. The failure mode of primary concern in mild to medium hard homogeneous steel plates subjected to normal impact is ductile failure. In this mode, as the missile penetrates the plate, plastically deformed material is pushed aside and petals or lips are formed on both the front and back faces with no material being ejected from the plate. For plates with Brinell hardness values above 300, failure by "plugging" is a strong possibility. In this brittle mode of failure, a plug of material is formed ahead of the penetrating missile and is ejected from the back side of the plate. A third mode of failure is disking or flaking, in which circular disks or irregular flakes are thrown from the back face. This type of failure is mainly a concern with plates of inferior quality steel and should not, therefore, be a common problem in the design of protective structures.



### 5 -49.2Primary Fragment Penetration Equations

In protective design involving primary fragments, a penetration equation is required which yields reliable estimates corresponding to the standard primary fragment illustrated in Figure 4-77 (+) of Chapter 4. These design equations consider only normal penetration which is critical for the design of protective structures. These equations apply to penetration into mild steel and are conservative for plates with a Brinell hardness value above 150. Steel penetration equations in design for primary fragment impact are expressed in the following forms:

For AP steel fragments penetrating mild steel plates,

$$\chi=0.30~W_{f}{}^{0.33}~V_{s}{}^{1.22}$$

and for mild steel fragments penetrating mild steel plates,

$$x=0.21~W_{f}^{0.33}~V_{s}^{1.22}$$

where 

x=depth of penetration (in)



$W_{f}=\mathrm{~}\mathit{f r a g m e n t~w e i g h t~}(o z)$ 

$V_{s}=$ striking velocity of fragment (kfps)



<!-- batch 29 | page 8 -->

Charts for steel penetration by primary fragments according to these equations are presented in Figure 5-28 (+) and Figure 5-29 (+).



To estimate the penetration of metal fragments other than armor piercing, the procedures outlined in Section 4-60.3 of Chapter 4 are entirely applicable to steel plates.



### 5 -49.3Residual Velocity After Perforation of Steel Plate

The penetration equations presented in Section 5-49.2 may be used for predicting the occurrence of perforation of metallic barriers and for calculating the residual fragment velocity after perforation.



For normal impact of a steel fragment, with the shape illustrated in Figure 4-77 (+) of Chapter 4, the equation for residual velocity is 

$$V_{r}\Lambda V_{s}=[1-(V_{x}\Lambda V_{s})^{2}]^{1/2}/(1+t/d)$$

where 

$V_{r}=$ residual velocity 

$V_{s}=$ striking velocity 

$V_{x}=$ critical perforation velocity for the fragment of impacting the plate of thickness t (see explanation below)



$d=$ diameter of cylindrical portion of fragment (in), as illustrated in Figure 4-77 (+) of Chapter 4



The value of $V_{x}$ is determined from Figure 5-28 (+) or Figure 5-29 (+) by substituting the plate thickness t for the penetration depth x and reading the corresponding value of striking velocity,$V_{s}$ .This striking velocity becomes the critical perforation velocity,$V_{x}$ .A plot of the residual velocity equation for a range of t/d ratios is presented in Figure 5-30(+).



Multiple plate penetration problems may be analyzed by the successive application of Equation 5-64 or 5-65 for predicting the depth of penetration and Equation 5-66 for calculating the residual velocity up on perforation of the plate. In addition, composite construction, consisting of concrete walls with attached spall plates, can be analyzed for fragment impact by tracing the motion of the fragment through each successive layer.The striking velocity of the fragment upon each intermediate layer is the residual fragment velocity after perforation of the previous layer. The conservative assumptions are made that the fragment remains intact during the penetration and that it does not deviate from a straight line path as it crosses the interface between the different media.

## TYPICAL DETAILS FOR BLAST-RESISTANT STEEL STRUCTURES 

## 5 -50GENERAL

This section presents several examples of typical framing connections, structural details and blast doors used in industrial installations designed to resist accidental blast loadings. This section is intended to augment those details presented in prior sections of this chapter.



## 5 -51

## STEEL FRAMED BUILDINGS

Such buildings are often rectangular in plan, two or three bays wide and four or more bays long. Figure 5-31 (+) shows an example of a typical framing plan for a single-story building designed to resist a pressure-time blast loading impinging on the structure at an angle with respect to its main axes. The structural system consists of an orthogonal network of rigid frames. The girders of the frames running parallel to the building length serve also as purlins and are placed, for ease of erection, on top of the frames spanning across the structure's width.



Figure 5-32 (+) to Figure 5-35 (+) present typical framing details related to the general layout of Figure 5-31 (+). As a rule, the columns are fabricated without splices, the plate covers and connection plates are shop welded to the columns, and all girder to column connections are field bolted. A channel is welded on top of the frame girders to cover the bolted connections and prevent (avoid) interference with the roof decking. All of the framing connections are designed to minimize stress concentrations and to avoid triaxial strains. They combine ductility with ease of fabrication.



## 5 -52

## COLD-FORMED,LIGHT GAGE STEEL PANELS

Figure 5-36 (+) shows typical cross sections of cold-formed, light gage steel panels commonly used in industrial installations. The closed sections, which are composed of a corrugated hat section and a flat sheet, are used to resist blast pressures in the low pressure range, whereas the open hat section is recommended only for very low pressure situations as siding or roofing material. A typical vertical section illustrates the attachment of the steel paneling to the supporting members. Of particular interest is the detail at the corner between the exterior wall and the roof, which is designed to prevent peeling of the decking that may be caused by negative pressures at the roof edge.

Figure 5-37 (+) gives some typical arrangements of welded connections for attaching cold-formed steel panels to their supporting elements. Type A refers to an intermediate support whereas Type B refers to an end support. It is recommended that the diameter of puddle welds be 3/4 of an inch minimum and should not exceed 1-1/2 inches because of space limitations in the panel valleys. For deeper panels, it is often necessary to provide two rows of puddle welds at the intermediate supports in order to resist the uplift forces in rebound. It should be noted that welds close to the hooked edge of the panel are recommended to prevent lifting of adjacent panels.

<!-- batch 29 | page 13 -->

Figure 5-38 (+) shows an arrangement of bolted connections for the attachment of coldformed steel panels to the structural framing. The bolted connection consists of: a threaded stud resistance welded to the supporting member, a square steel block with a concentric hold used as a spacer, and a washer and nut for fastening. Figure 5-39 (+)presents a cross section of that connection with all the relevant details along with information pertaining to puddle welds.



## 5 -53BLAST DOORS

Figure 5-40 (+) and Figure 5-41 (+) show details of single-leaf and double-leaf blast doors, respectively. Figure 5-40 (+) presents a single-leaf door installed in a steel structure. The design is typical of doors intended to resist relatively low pressure levels.It is interesting to note that the door is furnished with its tubing frame to ensure proper fabrication and to provide adequate stiffness during erection. In Figure 5-41 (+), the double-leaf door with its frame is installed in place and attached to the concrete structure. In both figures details of hinges, latches, anchors, and panic hardware are illustrated. It should be noted that the pins at the panic latch ends are made of aluminum in order to eliminate the danger of sparking, a hazard in ammunition facilities which might arise from steel-on-steel striking.



Figure 5-42 (+) shows details of compression arch and tension arch doors. The tension arch door requires compression ties to develop the compression reactions for the arch and to prevent the door from being blown through the opening. The compression arch door requires a tension tie plate to develop the reactions and to prevent large distortions in the door that may bind it in place.

# APPENDIX 5: STRUCTURAL STEEL  DESIGN 

<!-- batch 29 | page 27 -->

## APPENDIX 5AILLUSTRATIVE EXAMPLES 

This appendix presents detailed design procedures and numerical examples on the following topics:

1.Flexural elements subjected to pressure-time loading.

2.Lateral bracing requirements.

3.Cold-formed steel panels.

4.Columns and beam-columns.

5.Open-web joists.

6.Single-story rigid frames.

7.Blast doors.

8.Unsymmetrical bending.

References are made to the appropriate sections of this chapter and to charts, tables,and equations from Chapter 3 "Principles of Dynamic Analysis."

## PROBLEM 5A-1DESIGN OF BEAMS FOR PRESSURE-TIME LOADING 

Problem:Design of a purlin or girt as a flexural member which responds to a pressure-time loading.



## Procedure:

Step 1.Establish the design parameters:

a.Pressure-time load 

b.Design criteria: Maximum support rotation, Θ, depending on protection category 



C.Span length, L, beam spacing, b, and support conditions 

d.Properties and type of steel used, i.e.,$\pmb{f}_{y}$ and E 

Step 2.Determine the equivalent static load, w, using the following preliminary dynamic load factors as discussed in Section 5-22.3.

$$\begin{array}{r l}&{1.0\;\mathrm{f o r}\;\Theta=2^{\circ}}\\ {\mathrm{D L F}=}\\ &{0.5\;\mathrm{f o r}\;\Theta=12^{\circ}.}\end{array}$$

Step 3.Using the appropriate resistance formula from Table 3-1 (+) and the equivalent static load derived in Step 2, determine $M_{p}$ 

Step 4.Select a member size using Equation 5-7 or 5-8. Check the local buckling criteria of Section 5-24 for the member chosen.



Step 5.Determine the mass, m, including the weight of the decking over a distance center-to-center of purlins or girts, and the weight of the members.



Step 6.Calculate the equivalent mass $M_{e}$ using Table 3-12 (+) (Chapter 3).

Step 7.Determine the equivalent elastic stiffness $K_{E}$ from Table 3-8 (+)

Step 8.Calculate the natural period of vibration,$T_{N},$ using Equation 5-15.

Step 9.Determine the total resistance,$R_{u},$ and peak pressure load, P. Enter appropriate chart in Section 3-19.3 with the ratios $T/T_{N}$ and $P/R_{u}$ and the values of $C_{1}$ and $C_{2}$ in order to establish the ductility ratio $\nearrow$ 

Step 10.Check the assumed DIF used in Step 4. Enter the response charts with the ratio $T/T_{N}$ and $\mu$ and to determine $t_{E}$ Using Equation 5-1, determie the strain rate. Using Figure 5-2 (+), determine the DIF. If there is a significant difference from that assumed, repeat Steps 4 through 9.

<!-- batch 29 | page 29 -->

Step 11.Calculate the equivalent elastic deflection $X_{E}$ as given by the equation 

$$X_{F}=R_{u}/K_{F}$$

and establish the maximum deflection $X_{m}$ given by 

$$X_{m}=\mu X_{E}$$

Compute the corresponding member end rotation. Compare Θ with the criteria summarized in Section 5-35.



$$\tan\Theta=X_{m}/(L/2)$$

Step 12.Check for shear using equation 5-16 and Table 3-9 (+)

Step 13.If a different member size is required, repeat Steps 2 through 12 by selecting a new dynamic load factor.



<!-- batch 29 | page 30 -->

## EXAMPLE 5A-1DESIGN OF A BEAM FOR PRESSURE-TIME LOADING 

## Required:Design a simply supported beam for shear and flexure in a low pressure range where personnel protection is required

Step 1.Given:

a.Pressure-time loading (Figure 5A-1 (+))

b.Criteria:Personnel protection required. Support rotation limited to 2{\



C.Structural configuration (Figure 5A-1)

d.$f_{v}=36\ \mathrm{ksi},\ E=30\times10^{3}\ \mathrm{ksi},\ \mathrm{A36\ steel}$ 

e.Compression flange braced.

f.The girt supports a wall panel weighing 4.8 psf 

Figure 5A-1 Beam Configuration and Loading, Example 5A-1

<div style="text-align: center;"><img src="../pages/batch 29/imgs/img_in_image_box_159_815_1033_1066.jpg" alt="Image" width="73%" /></div>


Step 2.Determine the equivalent static load (i.e., required resistance). For this pressure range, the equivalent static load is assumed equal to the peak pressure (Section 5-22.3). The running load becomes:

$$w=1.0\times6.5\times4.5\times144/1000=4.21\mathrm{k/ft}$$

Step 3.Determine required $M_{P}$ 

$$M_{p}=\frac{wL^{2}}{8}=\frac{4.21\times172}{8}=152.1k-ft $$

Step 4.Select a member.

$$\left(S+Z\right)=\frac{2M_{p}}{f_{ds}}=\frac{2\times152.1\times12}{51.1}=71.4\ln^{3}$$

<!-- batch 29 | page 31 -->

where 

$$f_{ds}=SIF\times DIF\times f_v=1.1\times1.29\times36=51.1ksi $$

where 

$S I F=1.1$ from Section 5-13.2

$DIF=1.29$ corresponding to a DIF in the low pressure range (see Table 52(+))



$$\begin{aligned}&\mathsf{Select W12\times26},\quad S=33.4\mathsf{in^3}\quad I=204\mathsf{in^4}\\&Z=37.2\mathsf{in^3}\\&S+Z=70.6\mathsf{in^3}\\&M_p=(70.6\times51.1)/(2\times12)=150.3\mathsf{k}-\mathsf{ft}\\ \end{aligned}$$

Check local buckling criteria.

$$d/t_{w}=53.1<412/(f_{y})^{1/2}=68.7\mathrm{~O.K.}$$

Step 5.Calculate M.

$$M=\frac{wL}{g}=\frac{\left[(4.5\times4.8)+26\right]\left(17\times10^{6}\right)}{32.2\times1000}=25.130\left(k-ms^{2}\right)/ft $$

Step 6.Calculate the effective mass,$M_{\mathtt{e}},$ ,for a response in the elasto-plastic range.



$$\begin{aligned}&K_{LM}=(0.78+0.66)/2=0.72\\&M_e=0.72\times25,130=18,100\ k-ms^2/ft\\ \end{aligned}$$

Step 7.Determine $K_{E}$ 

$$K_{E}=\frac{384EI}{5L^{3}}=\frac{384\times30\times10^{3}\times204}{5\times17^{3}\times144}=664k/\pi $$

Step 8.Calculate $T_{N}$ 

$$T_{N}=2\pi(M_{e}/K_{E})^{1/2}=2\pi(18,100/664)^{1/2}$$

Step 9.Establish the ductility ratio  and compare with the criteria.

$$T/T_{N}=40/32.8=1.22$$

$$P=p\times L\times b=\frac{6.5\times17\times4.5\times144}{1000}=71.6\mathrm{kips}$$

<!-- batch 29 | page 32 -->

$$R_{u}=8M_{p}/L=(8\times150.3)/17=70.7\mathrm{~kips}$$

$$P/R_{u}=71.6/70.7=1.01$$

From Figure 3-64a (+),

$$\mu=X_{m}/X_{E}=2.1$$

At this point, the designer would check lateral bracing requirements.Sample problem 5A-2 outlines this procedure.



Step 10.Check the assumed DIF.

From Figure 3-64a (+), for $P/R_{u}=1.01\mathrm{a n d}T/T_{\mathrm{N}}=1.22$ 

$$t_{F}/T=0.24$$

$$t_{E}=0.24\times40=9.6ms $$

$$\dot{\varepsilon}=f_{ds}\left/\left(E_{s}t_{E}\right)\right/\left(E_{s}t_{E}\right)=51.1/30\times10^{3}\times0096=0.177\ln/\ln/\sec $$

From Figure 5-2 (+)

$$DIF=1.31=1.29\quad O.K.$$

Step 11.Determine XE:

$$\mathrm{X}_{\mathrm{E}}=\mathrm{R}_{\mathrm{u}}/\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{E}}=(70.7\times12)/664=1.28\text{inch}$$

Find Xm:

$$X_{m}=\mu X_{E}=2.1\times1.28=2.69inche\times $$

Find end rotation, .

$$\tan\theta=X_{\mathrm{m}}/(\mathrm{L}/2)=2.69/(8.5\times12)=0.0264$$

$$\Theta=1.52^{\circ}<2^{\circ}\quad0.K.$$

Step 12.Check shear.

Dynamic yield stress in shear 

$$f_{dv}=0.55f_{ds}=0.55\times51.1=28.1\mathrm{ksi}$$

Ultimate shear capacity 

$$V_{p}=f_{dv}\times A_{w}=28.1\times0.23\times12=77.6\ kips $$

Maximum support shear 

<!-- batch 29 | page 33 -->

$$\begin{aligned}&V_{s}=r_{u}\times L/2=R_{u}/2=70.7/2=35.4\mathrm{~kips};\\ &V_{p}>V_{s}\mathrm{~O.K}.\\ \end{aligned}$$

(Table 3-9 (+))

## PROBLEM 5A-2SPACING OF LATERAL BRACING 

### Problem:Investigate the adequacy of the lateral bracing specified for a flexural member. 

The design procedure for determining the maximum permissible spacing of lateral bracing is essentially a trial and error procedure if the unbraced length is determined by the consideration of lateral torsional buckling only. However, in practical design, the unbraced length is usually fixed by the spacing of purlins and girts and then must be investigated for lateral torsional buckling.



## Procedure:

Step 1.Establish design parameters.

a.Bending moment diagram obtained from a design analysis 

b.Unbraced length, 1, and radius of gyration of the member,$r_{y}$ , about its weak axis 



C.Dynamic design strength,$f_{d s}$ 

(Section 5-13)

d.Design ductility ratio,$\mu,$ from a design analysis 

Step 2.From the moment diagram, find the end moment ratio,$M/M_{0},$ ,for each segment of the beam between points of bracing. (Note that the end moment ratio is positive when the segment is bent in reverse curvature and negative when bent in single curvature).



Step 3.Compute the maximum permissible unbraced length,$I_{c r},$ using Equation 520 or 5-21, as applicable. Since the spacing of purlins and girts is usually uniform, the particular unbraced length that must be investigated in a design will be the one with the largest moment ratio. The spacing of bracing in nonyielded segments of a member should be checked against the requirements of the AISC Specification (see Section 5-26.3).

Step 4.The actual length of a segment being investigated should be less than or equal to $I_{c r}$ .



<!-- batch 29 | page 35 -->

$r_{v}=1.98$ inches 

C.Dynamic design ${\tt s t r e s s=51.1}$ ksi 

d.Design ductility ratio,.$\mu=5$ 

Step 2.The moment ratio is -0.5 for segments BC and CD (single curvature) and 0.5 for segments AB and DE (double curvature).

Step 3.Determine the maximum permissible unbraced length. By inspection,Equation 5-21 results in the lower value of $I_{c r}$ .,

$$\frac{\beta I_{c r}}{r_{v}}=\frac{1375}{f_{d s}}$$

Figure 5A-2 Bending Moment Diagram, Example 5A-2

From Figure 5-8 (+) for $x_{m}/x_{E}=5$ $\beta=1.36$ 

$$I_{cr}=\frac{1375\times1.98}{1.36\times51.1}=39.2in $$

A,E SUPPORTS 

B,C,D BRACING LOCATIONS 

Step 4.Since the actual unbraced length is less than 39.2 inches, the spacing of the bracing is adequate.

# PROBLEM 5A-3 DESIGN A ROOF DECK AS A FLEXURAL MEMBER WHICH  RESPONDS TO PRESSURE-TIME LOADING 

### Problem:Design of cold-formed, light gauge steel panels subjected to pressure-time loading. 

Step 1.Establish the design parameters:

a.Pressure-time loading 

b.Design criteria: Specify values ofμ and Θdepending upon whether tension-membrane action is present or not.



C.Span length and support conditions 

d.Mechanical properties of steel 

Step 2.Determine an equivalent uniformly distributed static load for a 1-ft width of panel, using the following preliminary dynamic load factors.


<div style="text-align: center;"><html><body><table border="1"><tr><td></td><td>Tension-membrane action not present</td><td>Tension-membrane action present</td></tr><tr><td>DLF</td><td>1.33</td><td>1.00</td></tr></table></body></html></div>


These load factors are based on an average value of $T/T_{N}=10.0$ , the recommended design ductility ratios. They are derived using Figure 3-64 (+/+) of Chapter 3.

$$w=\mathsf{DLF}\times p\times b $$

Equivalent static load 

$$b=1\;\mathrm{f t}$$

Step 3.Using the equivalent load derived in Step 2, determine the ultimate moment capacity using Equation 5-29 or 5-30 (assume positive and negative are the same).



Step 4.Determine required section moduli using Equation 5-27 or 5-28.

Select a panel.

Step 5.Determine actual section properties of the panel:

$\mathcal{S}^{+}$ S,/{20,W 

Step 6.Compute $r_{u},$ the maximum unit resistance per 1-ft width of panel using Equation 5-29 or 5-30.



<!-- batch 29 | page 37 -->

Step 7.Determine the equivalent elastic stiffness,$K_{E}=r_{u}L/X_{E}$ ,using Equation 531.



Step 8.Compute the natural period of vibration.

$$T_{N}=2\pi\left(0.74\;m L/K_{E}\right)^{1/2}$$

(Equation 5-32)

Step 9.Calculate $P/r_{u}$ and $T/T_{N}$ . Enter Figure 3-64$(\pm/\underline{{\pm}})$ with the ratios $P/r_{u}$ and $T/T_{N}$ to establish the actual ductility ratio $\mu$ 



Compare  with the criteria of Step 1. If $\mu$ is larger than the criteria value,repeat steps 4 to 9.



Step 10.Compute the equivalent elastic deflection $X_{E}$ using $X_{E}=r_{u}L/K_{E}$ _. Evaluate the maximum deflection,$X_{m}=\mu X_{E}$ 



Determine the maximum panel end rotation.

$$\Theta=\tan^{-1}\left[X_{m}/(L/2)\right]$$

Compare Θ with the criteria of step 1. If Θis larger than specified in the criteria, select another panel and repeat steps 5 to 10.

Step 11.Check resistance in rebound using Figure 5-13 (+).

Step 12.Check panel for maximum resistance in shear by applying the criteria relative to:

a.Simple shear, Table 5-5a (+), 5-6a (+) or 5-7a (+)

b.Combined bending and shear, Table 5-5b (+), 5-6b (+) or 5-7b (+)

C.Web crippling, Figures 5-15 (+) or 5-16 (+)

If the panel is inadequate in shear, select a new member and repeat steps 4 to 12.

# EXAMPLE 5A-3DESIGN A ROOF DECK AS A FLEXURAL MEMBERWHICH  RESPONDS TO PRESSURE-TIME LOADING 

### Required:Design a continuous cold-formed steel panel in a low pressure range. 

Step 1.Given:

a.Pressure-time loading (Figure 5A-3 (+))

b.Criteria: (Tension-membrane action present)

maximum ductility ratio $\mu_{m a x}=6$ 

maximum rotation $\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{m a x}}=4^{\circ}$ 

C.Structural configuration Figure 5A-3 (+)

d.Steel A446, grade a 

$\mathsf{E}=30\times10^6$ psi 

$$f_{ds}=SIF\times DIF\times f_v=1.21\times1.1\times33,000=44,000\mathrm{psi(Equation~5-26)}$$

Step 2.Determine the equivalent static load 

Say $\mathsf{D L F}=1.0$ and analyzing a one foot width of the panel 

$$w=DLF\times p\times b=1.0\times5.0\times12\times12=720\;lb/ft $$

Step 3.Determine required ultimate moment capacities. For preliminary selection,assume 



$$M_{up}=M_{un}=wL^{2}/10.8=720\times(4.5)^{2}/10.8=1,350\ lb-ft $$

(Equation 5-30)

<!-- batch 29 | page 39 -->

Figure 5A-3 Roof decking configuration and loading, Example 5A-3

<div style="text-align: center;"><img src="../pages/batch 29/imgs/img_in_image_box_206_328_1009_890.jpg" alt="Image" width="67%" /></div>


Step 4.Determine reguired section moduli.

$$S^{+}=S^{-}=(1350\times12)/44,000=0.368in^3$$

(Select Section 3-18, 1-1/2 inches deep)

Step 5.Determine actual section properties for a one foot width of panel.

From manufacturer's guide:

$$\begin{aligned}&S^{+}=0.398in^{3}\\&S^{-}=0.380in^{3}\\&J_{20}=0.337in^{4}\\&w=2.9psf\\ \end{aligned}$$

Step 6.Compute maximum unit resistance $r_{u}$ 

$$\begin{aligned}&M_{up}=(44,000\times0.398)/12=1,459\;\mathrm{lb}-\mathrm{ft}\\ &M_{un}=(44,000\times0.380)/12=1,393\;\mathrm{lb}-\mathrm{ft}\\ \end{aligned}$$

<!-- batch 29 | page 40 -->

$$r_{u}=3.6\frac{M_{un}+2M_{up}}{L^{2}}=3.6\frac{1.393+2\times1.459}{4.5^{2}}=766\ lb/ft $$

Step 7.Determine equivalent static stiffness.

$$K_{E}=\frac{r_{u}L}{X_{E}}=\frac{EI_{20}\times r_{u}\times L}{0.0062\times r_{u}\times L^{4}}=\frac{EI_{20}}{0.0062L^{3}}$$

$$\frac{30^{6}\times10\times0.337}{0.0062\times4.5^{3}\times144}=124,260\mathrm{lb/ft}$$

Step 8.

Compute the natural period of vibration for the 1-ft width of panel.

$$\begin{aligned}&mL=w/g=(2.9\times10^{6}\times4.5)/32.2=4.05\times10^{5}\mathrm{lb}-\mathrm{ms}^{2}/\mathrm{ft}\\ &T_{N}=2\times\left[(0.74\times4.05\times105)/124,260\right]^{1/2}=9.75\mathrm{~msec}\\ \end{aligned}$$

Step 9.Calculate $P/r_{u}$ and $T/T_{N}$ 

$$\begin{aligned}&P=p\times b=5.0\times12\times12=720\;\mathrm{lb/ft}\\&P/r_{u}=720/766=0.94\\&T/T_{N}=40/9.75=4.10\\ \end{aligned}$$

Entering Figure 3-64a (+) with these values.

$$X_{m}/X_{F}=3.5<6\quad O.K.$$

Step 10.Check maximum deflection and rotation.

$$\begin{aligned}&X_{E}=r_{u}L/K_{E}=766\times4.5/124,260=0.028ft\\ &X_{m}=3.5X_{E}=0.098ft\\ \end{aligned}$$

$$\Theta=\tan^{-1}\left[X_m/(L/2)\right]=\tan^{-1}\left[0.098/2.25\right]=2.5<4^{\circ}$$

Step 11.Check resistance in rebound.

From Figure $5-13\text{(+)}$ $\bar{r}/r=0.33;\mathrm{O.K}$ . since available maximum elastic resistance in rebound is approximately equal to that under direct loading.Step 12.Check resistance in shear.

a.Interior support (combined shear and bending). Determine dynamic shear capacity of a 1-ft width of panel:

$$\begin{aligned}&h=(1.500-2t)inches,t=0.048inch\\ &\quad=1.500-0.096=1.404inches\\ &\quad h/t=1.404/0.048=29.25=30\\ \end{aligned}$$

<!-- batch 29 | page 41 -->

$$f_{dv}=10.84\mathrm{ksi}$$

(Table 5-5 (+))

Total web area for 1-ft width of panel:

$$(8\times h\times t)/2=4\times1.404\times0.048=0.270in^2$$

$$V_{u}=0.270\times10.84=2.92\ k=2.922\ lb $$

Determine maximum equivalent static shear force.

The maximum shear at an interior support of a continuous panel using limit design is:

$$\begin{aligned}V_{max}&=0.55\ r_{u}L=0.55\times766\times4.5=1,896\ lb\\&=1.896\ lb<2,922\ lb\quad O.K.\end{aligned}$$

### b.End support (simple shear)

Determine dynamic shear capacity of a 1-ft width of panel:

$$\begin{aligned}&For h/t\leq57,\quad f_{dv}=0.50f_{ds}=0.5\times44.0=22.0\quad ksi\quad(Table5-5a(+))\\&V_u=0.270\times22.000=5.940\\ \end{aligned}$$

Determine maximum dynamic shear force:

The maximum shear at an end support of a continuous panel using limit design is 



$$\begin{aligned}V_{max}&=0.45\times r_u\times L=0.45\times766\times4.5\\&=1.551\;|\mathsf{b}<5.940\;|\mathsf{b}\quad\mathsf{O}.\mathsf{K}.\end{aligned}$$

C.Web crippling (4 webs per foot)

End support.$(N=2-\%inchees)$ 

$$Q_{\mathrm{u}}=1.200\times4=4.800\mathrm{~lb}>1.551\mathrm{~O.K.}$$

Interior support.$(N=5inchees)$ ！

$$Q_{u}=(2,400\times4)/2=4,800\mathrm{~lb}>1,896\mathrm{~O.K.}$$

## PROBLEM 5A-4DESIGN OF COLUMNS AND BEAM-COLUMNS 

### Problem:Design a column or beam-column for axial load combined with bending about the strong axis. 

## Procedure:

Step 1.Establish design parameters.

a.Bending moment M, axial load P, and shear V are obtained from either a preliminary design analysis or a computer analysis.

b.Span length / and unbraced lengths $I_{x}$ and $I_{v^{\prime}}$ ,

C.Properties of structural steel:

Minimum yield strength $\pmb{f}_{y}$ 

Dynamic increase factor $D I F$ 

(Table 5-2 (+))

Dynamic design strength $f_{d s}$ 

(Equation 5-2)

Step 2.Select a preliminary member size with a section modulus S such that $S>M/f_{d s}$ : and bf/2tf complies with the structural steel being used (Section 5-24).



Step 3.Calculate $P_{v}$ (Section 5-24) and the ratio $P/P_{v}$ .Using either Equation 5-17or 5-18, determine the maximum allowable $\dot{d}t_{w}$ ratio and compare it to that of the section chosen. If the allowable $d/t_{w}$ ratio is less than that of the trial section, choose a new trial section.



Step 4.Check the shear capacity of the web. Determine the web area $A_{w}$ (Section 5-23) and the allowable dynamic shear stress $f_{d v}$ (Equation 5-4).Calculate the web shear capacity $V_{p}$ (Equation 5-16) and compare to the design shear V. If inadequate, choose a new trial section and return to Step 3.



Step 5.Determine the radii of gyration,$r_{x}$ and $r_{y},$ and plastic section modulus, Z, of the trial section from the AlSC Handbook.



Step 6.Calculate the following quantities using the various design parameters:

a.Equivalent plastic resisting moment 

$$M_{0}=f_{d s}Z r 
$$

(Equation 5-8)

b.Effective slenderness ratios $K I_{x}/r_{x}$ and $K I_{v}/r_{v};$ ;for the effective length factor K, see appropriate Commentary on the AISC Specification and Section 5-38



C.Allowable axial stress $F_{a}$ corresponding to the larger value of Kl/r 

<!-- batch 29 | page 43 -->

d.Allowable moment $M_{m}$ from Equation 5-47 or 5-48

e.$F_{e}$ and "Euler" buckling load $P_{e}$ (Section 5-37.3)

f.Plastic axial load (Section 5-37.3) and ultimate axial load $P_{u}$ (Equation 5-42)



g.Coefficient $C_{m}$ (AISC Specification)

Step 7.Using the quantities obtained in Step 6 and the applied moment M and axial load P, check the interaction formulas (Equations 5-44 and 5-45).Both formulas must be satisfied for the trial section to be adequate.

$$\begin{aligned}&a.\quad&M_{px}=f_{ds}×Z_x=51.1×43.1×1/12=183.5ft-kips\quad&(Equation5-8)\\&b.\quad&K=0.75\quad&(Section5-39)\\&\quad&KJ_x/r_x=[0.75(17)12]/5.21=29\\&\quad&KJ_y/r_y=[0.75(17)12]/1.52=101\\&c.\quad&F_a=12.85ksi for KJ_y/r_y=101and f_y=36ksi\end{aligned}$$

(AISC Specification)

d.

$$\left[1.07-\frac{(204/152)\times51.1^{1/2}}{3.160}\right]\times183.5=140.6<183.5\mathrm{ft}-\mathrm{kips}$$

e.

$$F_{ex}=\frac{12\pi^{2}E}{23\left(KI_{b}/r_{x}\right)^{2}}=\frac{12\pi^{2}\times29.000}{23\times29^{2}}=177.6\mathrm{ksi}$$

## PROBLEM 5A-5DESIGN OF OPEN-WEB STEEL JOISTS 

### Problem:Analysis or design of an open-web joist subjected to a pressure-time loading. 

## Procedure:

Step 1.Establish design parameters 

a.Pressure-time curve 

b.Clear span length and joist spacing 

C.Minimum yield stress $f_{y}$ for chord and web members 

Dynamic increase factor, DIF 

d.Design ductility ratio, , and maximum end rotation, .

e.Determine whether joist design is controlled by maximum end reaction.



### Step 2.Select a preliminary joist size as follows:

a.Assume a dynamic load factor (Section 5-22.3)

b.Compute equivalent static load on joist due to blast overpressure 

$$w_{1}=\mathsf{D L F}\times\mathsf{p}\times\mathsf{b}$$

(Dead load of joist and decking not included)

C.Equivalent service live load on joist 

$$w_{2}=w_{1}/1.7\times S I F\times D I F $$

(Section 5-33)

d.From "Standard Load Tables" adopted by the Steel Joist Institute,select a joist for the given span and the structural steel being used,with a allowable design load (dead load of joist and decking excluded) equal to or greater than W2, check whether ultimate capacity of joist is controlled by flexure or by shear 

Step 3.

Find the resistance of the joist by multiplying the allowable design load by $1.7\times S I F\times D I F$ (Section 5-33)Step 4.Calculate the stiffness of the joist,$K_{E}$ , using Table 3-8 (+). Determine the equivalent elastic deflection $X_{E}$ given by 

$$X_{E}=r_{u}L/K_{E}$$

<!-- batch 29 | page 51 -->

Step 5.Determine the effective mass using the weight of the joist with its tributary area of decking, and the corresponding load-mass factor given in Table 3-12 (+) of Chapter 3.



Calculate the natural period of vibration,$T_{N}$ 

Follow procedure outlined in Step 6a or 6b depending on whether the joist capacity is controlled by flexure or by shear.



Joist design controlled by flexure.

a.Find ductility ratio,$\mu=X_{m}/X_{E}$ :from the response charts in Chapter 3,using the values of $T/T_{N}$ and $P/r_{u}$ 



b.Check if the ductility ratio and maximum end rotation meet the criteria requirements outlined in Section 5-35.

If the above requirements are not satisfied, select another dynamic load factor and repeat Steps 2 to 5.



C.Check the selection of the dynamic increase factor used in Step 2c.Using the response charts, find $t_{E}$ to determine the strain rate, ε in Equation 5-1. Using Figure 5-2 (+), determine DIF.

d.Check if the top chord meets the requirements for a beam-column (Section 5-37.3)Joist design controlled by shear.

a.Find ductility ratio $\mu=X_{m}/X_{E}$ ·from the response charts in Chapter 3,using the values of $T/T_{N}$ and $P/r_{u}$ 



b.If $\mu\leq1.0$ , design is O.K.

If $\mu\ge1.0$ ,assume a higher dynamic load factor and repeat Steps 2to 5. Continue until $\mu\le1.0$ . Check end rotation, Θ, against design criteria.



C.Check the selection of the dynamic increase factor used in Step 2c,using the value of $T/T_{N}$ and the appropriate elastic response chart in Section 3-19.3.



d.Since the capacity is controlled by maximum end reaction, it will generally not be necessary to check the top chord as a beamcolumn. However, when such a check is warranted, the procedure in Step 6a can be followed.



Check the bottom chord for rebound.

a.Determine the required resistance, , for elastic behavior in rebound.



<!-- batch 29 | page 52 -->

b.Compute the bending moment, M, and find the axial forces in top and bottom chords using $P=M/d$ where d is taken as the distance between the centroids of the top and bottom chord sections.

C.Determine the ultimate axial load capacity of the bottom chord considering the actual slenderness ratio of its elements.

$$P_{u}=1.7A F_{a}$$

where $F_{a}$ is defined in Section 5-37.3.

The value of $F_{a}$ can be obtained by using either Equation 5-43 or the tables in the AISC Specification which give allowable stresses for compression members. When using these tables, the yield stress should be taken equal to $f_{d s}$ ∴



### d.Check if $P_{u}>P_{1}$ 

Determine bracing requirements.

EXAMPLE 5A-5 (A)DESIGN OF AN OPEN-WEB STEEL JOIST 

Required:Design a simply-supported open-web steel joist whose capacity is controlled by flexure.



## Solution:

Step 1.Given:

a.Pressure-time loading 

(Figure 5A-4 (+))

b.Clear span =50'-0"

Spacing of joists = 7'-0"

Weight of decking  = 4 psf 

C.Structural steel properties 

Chords $f_{v}=50,000\;\mathrm{p s i}$ 

Web 

$$f_{v}=36,000\mathrm{p s i}$$

Dynamic increase factor (chords only).

$$\mathsf{D I F}=1.19$$

(Table 5-2 (+), for A588)

Dynamic design stress,$\boldsymbol{f}_{d s}=\boldsymbol{D I F}\times\boldsymbol{S I F}\times\boldsymbol{f}_{V}$ (Equation 5-2)

Chords $f_{ds}=1.19\times1.1\times50,000=65,450\mathrm{psi}$ 

d.Design criteria 

(Section 5-35)

Maximum ductility ratio: μmax =4.0

Maximum end rotation:$\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{m a x}}\qquad=2^{\circ}$

### Step 2.Selection of joist size 

a.Assume a dynamic load factor. For preliminary design, a $\mathsf{D L F}=1.0$ is generally recommended. However, since the span is quite long in this case,a DLF of 0.62 is selectd.



b.Equivalent static load on joist:

$$w_{1}=0.62\times2.0\times144\times7.0=1.250\mathrm{~lb/ft}$$

C.Corresponding allowable design load on joist:

$$w_{2}=w_{1}/(1.7\times1.19\times1.1)=1.250/2.23=561\ \mathrm{lb/ft}$$

d.Using the "Standard Specifications, Load Tables and Weight Tables" of the Steel Joist Institute, for a span of 50'-O", try 32LH11.Joist tables show that capacity is controlled by flexure.

Total load-carrying capacity (including dead load $)=602$ Ib/ft 

Approximate weight of joist and decking 

$$28+(4\times7)=56\ lb/\mathrm{ft}$$

Total allowable loadcapacity (excluding dead $\mathrm{load})=602-56=$ 546 Ib/ft 



The following section properties refer to the selected joist 32LH11(Figure 5A-4 (+)):

Top Chord:

$$\begin{aligned}&Two3×3×5/16angles\\ &A=3.56in^2\\ &r_x=0.92in\\ &r_y=1.54in\\ &I_x=3.02in^4\\ \end{aligned}$$

Bottom Chord:

$$\begin{aligned}&Two3×2-1/2×1/4angles\\&A=2.62in^2\\&r_x=0.945in\\&r_y=1.28in\\&I_x=2.35in^4\\&I_{xx}for joist=1,383.0in^4\\&Panel length=51inches\\ \end{aligned}$$

Note: If the moment of inertia for the joist $(\mathsf{I}_{\mathsf{x}\mathsf{x}})$ is not available, it may be estimated from the load causing a given deflection to span ratio (i.e. midspan deflection equal to L/240)stated in the "Standard Specifications, Load Tables and Weight Tables" for the given joist size and span, the corresponding midspan deflection, the elastic modulus for steel,and the theoretical deflection equation for a beam in flexure with simple supports.However, care should be taken for short spans where the allowable load in the "Standard Specifications, Load Tables and Weight Tables" may not be not controlled by flexural response.



Step 3.Resistance per unit length 

$$r_{u}=1.7\times1.19\times1.1\times546=1215\text{lb/ft}$$

Step 4.

$$K_{E}=\frac{384EI}{5L^{3}}=\frac{384\times29\times10^{6}\times1383}{5\times(12\times50)^{3}}=14.260\ lb/ln $$

<!-- batch 30 | page 4 -->

$$X_{E}=\frac{r_{u}L}{K_{E}}=\frac{1215\times50}{14260}=4.26\text{inches}$$

Step 5.Total mass of joist plus decking 

$$M=\frac{56\times50\times10^{6}}{386}=7.25\times10^{6}\mathrm{~lb}-\mathrm{ms}^{2}/\mathrm{in}$$

Total effective mass $M_{e}=K_{L M}M$ 

$$K_{LM}=0.5(0.78+0.66)=0.72$$

(Table 3-12 (+))

$$M_{e}=0.72\left(7.25\times10^{6}\right)=5.22\times10^{6}lb-ms^{2}/in $$

Natural period $T_{N}=2\pi(M_{e}/K_{E})^{1/2}=2\pi(5,220,000/14,260)^{1/2}=120.2\ ms$ 

Behavior controlled by flexure. Use Step 6a.

Step 6a.

a.

$$\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{N}}=40/120.2=0.332$$

$$\frac{P}{r_{u}}=\frac{2.0\times144\times7}{1.215}=1.66$$

From Figure 3-64a (+),

$$\mu=X_{m}/X_{E}=1.6<4\quad O.K.$$

b.

$$X_{m}=1.6\times3.87=6.2\text{inches}$$

$$\tan\theta=X_{m}/(L/2)=6.2/(25\times12)=0.021$$

$$\Theta=1.2^{\circ}<2^{\circ}\quad O.K.$$

C.Check selection of DIF.

From Figure 3-64a (+), for.$\mu=1.6\mathrm{a n d}T/T_{N}=0.33$ 

$$t_{F}/T=0.65,\ t_{F}=0.65\times40=26\ ms I 
$$

Findε

$$Findε
\dot{\varepsilon}=f_{ds}/E_{s}t_{E}=65.45/30\times10^{3}\times0.026=0.117\ in/in/sec(Equation5-1)$$

From Figure 5-2 (+) (average of A36 and A514)

$DIF=1.18=1.19$ assumed 

O.K.

d.Check top chord as a beam column.

Maximum moment at mid-span 

<!-- batch 30 | page 5 -->

$$M=\frac{r_{u}L^{2}}{8}=\frac{1.105\times50^{2}\times12}{8\times1000}=4.144in-kips $$

Maximum axial load in chords 

$$P=M/d $$

$$\begin{array}{r}{d=\mathsf{d i s t a n c e b e t w e e n c e n t r o i d s o f t o p a n d b o t t o m c h o r d s}}\\ {(\mathsf{s e e F i g u r e5A-4(\underline{{+}})})}\end{array}$$

$$30.22inchees $$

$$P=4.144/30.22=137.1\mathrm{kips}$$

$$I=\mathrm{panel}\mathrm{length}=51\mathrm{inches}$$

Slenderness ratio,$I/r_{x}=51/0.92=55.4<C_{c}$ 

where $C_{c}=(2\pi^{2}E/f_{d s})^{1/2}=95$ 

$$F_{a}=23.5\ \mathrm{ksi}\ \mathrm{for}\ f_{v}=50\ \mathrm{ksi}$$

(AISC Specification)

$$1.31\ (23.5)=30.8\ \mathrm{ksi}\ \mathrm{for}\ f_{ds}=65,450\ \mathrm{psi}$$

$$P_{u}=1.7AF_{a}=1.7\times3.56\times30.8=186.4\ kips $$

Considering the first panel as a fixed, simply supported beam, the maximum moment in the panel is 

$$M=\frac{r_{u}L^{2}}{12}=\frac{1105\times51^{2}}{12\times12\times1000}=19.96in-kips $$

The effective slenderness ratio of the top chord in the first panel is 

$$K I_{b}/r_{x}=(1.0\times51)/0.92=55.4$$

$$F_{ex}=\frac{12\pi^{2}E}{23\left(KI_{b}/r_{x}\right)^{2}}=\frac{12\pi^{2}\times29.000}{23\times55.4^{2}}=48.7\ \mathrm{ksh}$$

$$P_{ex}=(23/12)AF_{ex}=23/12\times3.56\times48.7=333kips $$

$$(1-P/P_{e x})=(1.0-126.6/333)=0.62$$

To determine $M_{m},$ the plastic moment $M_{P}$ is needed and the value of $Z_{x}$ has to be computed. The neutral axis for a fully plastic section is located at a distance x from the flange.



<!-- batch 30 | page 6 -->

$$\begin{aligned}3\overline{x}&=\left(3-\frac{5}{16}\right)\frac{5}{16}+3\left(\frac{5}{16}-\overline{x}\right)\\&=\left(43\times5\right)/\left(16\times16\right)+15/16-3\overline{x}\\\overline{x}&=455/\left(6\times256\right)=0.296\mathrm{inch}\end{aligned}$$

The plastic section modulus,$Z_{x},$ is found to be 

$$Z_{x}=2\left[\frac{0.296^{2}}{2}\times3+(3.0-0.3125)\times\frac{(0.3125-0.296)^{2}}{2}+\frac{(3-0.296)^{2}}{2}\times0.3125\right]$$

$$M_{px}=f_{ds}Z_x=65.45\times2.549=166.8\ in-kips $$

$$M_{mx}=\left[1.07-\frac{\left(I/r_y\right)f_{ds}^{1/2}}{3160}\right]M_{px}\leq M_{px}$$

$$\begin{aligned}&\mathrm{where}\ r_{y}\ \mathrm{is\ least\ r\_a\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_}\\ &=[1.07-(55.4/391)]\ 166.8=154.8\ \mathrm{in\_kips}\\ &C_{m}=0.85\ \mathrm{(AlSC\_Specifically)}\\ &\mathrm{(P/P_{u}+C_{m}M/[(1-P/P_{ex})M_{mx}]\leq1.10\ (Equation\ 5-44))}\\ &\mathrm{(\frac{137.1}{186.4}+\frac{0.85\times19.96}{0.62\times154.8}\leq1.0=0.736+0.176=0.912<1.0\ \mathrm{O.K}}\\ \end{aligned}$$

Step 7.Check bottom chord for rebound.

a.Calculate required resistance in rebound.

$$\mathsf{T}/\mathsf{T}_{\mathsf{N}}=0.33$$

From Figure 5-13 (+), 100% rebound 

$$\begin{aligned}&\bar{r}/r_{u}=1.0\\&\bar{r}=r_{u}=1.105\mathrm{lb/ft}\\ \end{aligned}$$

b.Moment and axial forces in rebound 

$$M=\frac{\overline{r}L^{2}}{8}=4,144\ln-\mathrm{kips}$$

Maximum axial force in bottom chord 

$$P=M/d=137.1\;\mathrm{k i p s}\;(\mathrm{c o m p r e s s i o n})$$

<!-- batch 30 | page 7 -->

C.Ultimate axial load capacity 

Stability in vertical direction (about x-axis)

$$I=51inchees\quad r_x=0.945$$

$$I/r=51/0.945=54.0<C_{c}$$

$$where$C_{c}=[(2\pi^{2}E)/f_{d s}]^{1/2}=\;95$ $$

$$F_{a}=23.72\ \mathrm{ksi}\ \mathrm{for}\ f_{v}=50\ \mathrm{ksi}\ \mathrm{(AlSC\_specification)}$$

$$1.31(23.72)=31.1\ \mathrm{ksi}\ \mathrm{for}\ f_{ds}=65,450\ \mathrm{psi}$$

$$P_{u}=1.7AF_{a}=1.7\times2.62\times31.1=138.5\ kips $$

d.Check bracing requirements.

$$P=137.1<P_u=138.5\quad0.K.$$

Adding a vertical member between panel joints of bottom chord would have been required had $P>P_{u}$ . This additional bracing would have been needed in mid-span but may be spared at the  joist ends.



Stability in the lateral direction (about y-axis)

$$P_{u}=137.1\ kips $$

$$r_{v}=1.28\ inches,\quad A=2.62\ in^{2}$$

$$F_{a}=P_{u}/(1.7\times1.31A)=137.1/(1.7\times1.31\times2.62)=23.5\mathrm{ksi}$$

For a given $F_{a}=23.5$ , the corresponding slenderness ratio is 

$$1/r\approx55$$

(AISC Specification)

Therefore, the maximum unbraced length in mid-span is 

$$J_{b}=55\times1.28=70.4inches $$

Use lateral bracing at panel points, i.e., 51 inches at midspan. The unbraced length may be increased at joist ends, but not greater than specified for bridging requirements in the joist specification.

EXAMPLE 5A-5 (B)ANALYSIS OF EXISTING OPEN-WEB STEEL JOIST 

Required:Analyze a simply supported, open-web steel joist whose capacity is controlled by shear.



## Solution:

Step 1.Given:

a.Pressure-time loading [Figure 5A-5 (+)]

Joist 22H11

b.Clear span = 32'-0"

Spacing of joists = 6'-0"

Weight of decking = 4 psf 

Figure 5A-5 Joist Cross-Section and Loading, Example 5A-5(b)

<div style="text-align: center;"><img src="../pages/batch 30/imgs/img_in_image_box_251_744_919_1211.jpg" alt="Image" width="56%" /></div>


C.Properties of structural steel:

Chords 

Web 

$$f_{y}=50,000\;\mathrm{p s i}$$

Dynamic increase factor 

(chords only)

$DIF=1.19$ (Table 5-2 (+) for A588)

Dynamic design stress $\boldsymbol{f}_{d s}=\boldsymbol{D I F}\times\boldsymbol{S I F}\times\boldsymbol{f}_{v}$ 

<!-- batch 30 | page 9 -->

Chords 

$$f_{ds}=1.19\times1.1\times50,000=65,450\mathrm{psi}$$

d.Design criteria 

(Section 5-35)

For members controlled by shear:

$$\begin{aligned}&\mu_{max}=1.0\\&\varTheta_{max}=1^{\circ}\\ \end{aligned}$$

Step 2.

a.Assume the $\mathsf{D L F}=1.25$

# PROBLEM 5A-6DESIGN OF SINGLE-STORY RIGID FRAMES FOR PRESSURE TIME LOADING 

Problem:Design a single-story, multi-bay rigid frame subjected to a pressuretime loading.



## Procedure:

Step 1.Establish the ratio α between the design values of the horizontal and vertical blast loads.



Step 2.Using the recommended dynamic load factors presented in Section 5-41.3establish the magnitude of the equivalent static load w for:

a.Local mechanisms of the roof and blastward column 

b.Panel or combined mechanisms for the frame as a whole.

Step 3.Using the general expressions for the possible collapse mechanisms from Table 5-13 (+) and the loads from Step 2, assume values of the moment capacity ratios C and $C_{1}$ and proceed to establish the required design plastic moment $M_{P}$ considering all possible mechanisms. In order to obtain a reasonably economical design, it is desirable to select C and $C_{1}$ so that the least resistance (or the required value of $M_{p})$ corresponds to a combined mechanism. This will normally require several trials with assumed values of C and $C_{1}$ 



Step 4.Calculate the axial loads and shears in all members using the approximate method of Section 5-41.4.



Step 5.Design each member as a beam-column using the ultimate strength design criteria of Sections 5-37.3, 5-38, and 5-39.A numerical example is presented in Problem 5A-4.



Step 6.Using the moments of inertia from Step 5, calculate the sidesway natural period using Table 5-14 (+) and Equations 5-50 and 5-51. Enter the response charts in Chapter 3 with the ratios of $T/T_{N}$ and $P/R_{u}$ . In this case,$P/R_{u}$ is the reciprocal of the panel or sidesway mechanism dynamic load factor used in the trial design. Multiply the ductility ratio by the elastic deflection given by Equation 5-53 and establish the peak deflection $X_{m}$ from Equation 5-54. Compare the maximum sidesway deflection $X_{m}$ ih the criteria of Section 5-35. Note that the sidesway deflection δin Table 58$(+)$ ,is $X_{m}$ ..1.



Step 7.Repeat the procedure of Step 6 for the local mechanisms of the roof and blastward column. The stiffness and natural period may be obtained from Table 3-8 (+) of Chapter 3 and Equation 5-15, respectively. The resistance of the roof girder and the blastward column may be obtained from Table 5

<!-- batch 30 | page 14 -->

13 (+) using the values of $M_{P}$ and $C M_{p}$ determined in Step 3. Compare the ductility ratio and rotation with the criteria of Section 5-35.

## Step 8

a.If the deflection criteria for both sidesway and beam mechanisms are satisfied, then the member sizes from Step 5 constitute the results of this preliminary design. These members would then be used in a more rigorous dynamic frame analysis. Several computer programs are available through the repositories listed in Section 5-4.



b.If the deflection criterion for a sidesway mechanism is exceeded,then the resistance of all or most of the members should be increased.



C.If the deflection criterion for a beam mechanism of the front wall or roof girder is exceeded, then the resistance of the member in question should be increased. The member sizes to be used in a final analysis should be the greater of those determined from Steps 8b and 8c.

## PROBLEM 5A-7DESIGN OF DOORS FOR PRESSURE-TIME LOADING 

### Problem:Design a steel-plate blast door subjected to a pressure-time loading.Procedure:

Step 1.Establish the design parameters 

a.Pressure-time load 

b.Design criteria: Establish support rotation,$\varTheta_{m a x}$ ,and whether seals and rebound mechanisms are required 

C.Structural configuration of the door including geometry and support conditions 



d.Properties of steel used:

Minimum yield strength,$\pmb{f}_{y}$ , for door components (Table 5-1 (+))

Dynamic increase factor, DIF (Table 5-2 (+))Step 2.Select the thickness of the plate.

Step 3.Calculate the elastic section modulus, S, and the plastic section modulus,Z, of the plate.



Step 4.Calculate the design plastic moment,$M_{p},$ of the plate (Equation 5-7)

Step 5.Compute the ultimate dynamic shear,$V_{p}$ 

Step 6.Calculate maximum support shear, V, using a dynamic load factor of 1.25and determine $V/V_{p}$ .lf $V/V_{p}$ is less than 0.67, use the plastic design moment as computed in Step 4 (Section 5-31). If $V/V_{p}$ is greater than 0.7,use Equation 5-23 to calculate the effective $M_{P}$ 



Step 7.Calculate the ultimate unit resistance of the section (Table 3-1$(\pm))$ __,using the equivalent plastic moment as obtained in Step 4 and a dynamic load factor of 1.25.



Step 8.Determine the moment of inertia of the plate section.

Step 9.Compute the equivalent elastic unit stiffness,$K_{E}$ , of the plate section.

(Table 3-8 (+))

Step 10.Calculate the equivalent elastic deflection,$X_{E}$ , of the plate as given by $X_{E}=r_{u}/K_{E}$ :

Step 11.Determine the load-mass factor $K_{L M}$ and compute the effective unit mass,

$m_{e}$ 



Step 12.Compute the natural period of vibration,$T_{N}$ 

<!-- batch 30 | page 23 -->

Step 13.Determine the door plate response using the values of $P/r_{u}$ and $T/T_{N}$ and the response charts of Chapter 3. Determine $X_{m}/X_{E}$ and $T_{E}$ 

Step 14.Determine the support rotation,

tan $\Theta=(X_{m})/(L/2)$ 

Compare  with the design criteria of Step 1b.

Step 15.Determine the strain rate, ε, using Equation 5-1. Determine the dynamic increase factor using Figure 5-2 (+) and compare with the DIF selected in Step 1d.



If the criteria of Step 1 is not satisfied, repeat Steps 2 to 15 with a new plate thickness.



Step 16.Design supporting flexural element considering composite action with the plate (if so constructed).



Step 17.Calculate elastic and plastic section moduli of the combined section.

Step 18.Follow the design procedure for a flexural element as described in Section 5A-1.

## EXAMPLE 5A-7 (A)DESIGN OF A BLAST DOOR FOR PRESSURE-TIME LOADING 

Required:Design a double-leaf, built-up door (6 ft by 8 ft) for the given pressure-time loading.



### Step 1.Given:

a.Pressure-time loading 

(Figure 5A-7 (+))

b.Design criteria: This door is to protect personnel from exterior loading. Leakage into the structure is permitted but the maximum end rotation of any member is limited to $2^{\circ}$ c 

C.Structural configuration (Figure 5A-7 (+))

Note:

This type of door configuration is suitable for low-pressure range applications.



d.Steel used:A36

Figure 5A-7 Door Configuration and Loading, Example 5A-7(a)

<div style="text-align: center;"><img src="../pages/batch 30/imgs/img_in_image_box_213_806_984_1317.jpg" alt="Image" width="64%" /></div>


Yield strength,$f_{y}=36$ ksi (Table 5-1 (+))Dynamic increase factor,$DIF=1.29$ (Table 5-2 (+))Average yield strength increase factor,$S I F=1.1$ (Section 5-12.1)Hence, the dynamic design stress,

<!-- batch 30 | page 25 -->

$$f_{ds}=1.1\times1.29\times36=51.1\mathrm{ksi}$$

and the dynamic yield stress in shear,

$$f_{dv}=0.55f_{ds}=0.55\times51.1=28.1\mathrm{ksi}$$

Step 2.Assume a plate thickness of 5/8 inch.

Step 3.Determine the elastic and plastic section moduli (per unit width).

$$\frac{bd^{2}}{6}=\frac{1\times(5/8)^{2}}{6}=6.515\times10^{-2}\ln^{3}/\ln^{2}$$

Step 4.Calculate the design plastic moment,$M_{p}$ 

$$\begin{aligned}&M_{p}=f_{ds}\left(S+Z\right)/2=51.1\left[\left(6.515\times10^{-2}\right)\right.\\ &\left.\quad+\left(9.765\times10^{-2}\right)\right]/2=51.1\times8.14\times10^{-2}=4.16\ in-k/in\\ \end{aligned}$$

Step 5.Calculate the dynamic ultimate shear capacity,$V_{p},$ ,for a 1-inch width.

$$V_{p}=f_{dv}A_{w}=28.1\times1\times5/8=17.56\ kips/in $$

Step 6.Evaluate the support shear and check the plate capacity. Assume 

$D L F=1.25$ 



$$V=DLF\times P\times L/2=\frac{1.25\times14.8\times36\times1}{2}=333\ lb/in=0.333\ kPa/in $$

$$V/V_{p}=0.333/17.56=0.0190<0.67$$

No reduction in equivalent plastic moment is necessary.

Note:

When actual DLF is determined, reconsider Step 6.

Step 7.Calculate the ultimate unit resistance,$r_{u}$ , (assuming the plate to be simplysupported at both ends).



$$r_{u}=\frac{8M_{p}}{L^{2}}=\frac{8\times4.16\times10^{3}}{36^{2}}=25.7\ ps $$

Step 8.Compute the moment of inertia, 1, for a 1-inch width.

$$I=\frac{bd^{3}}{12}=\frac{1\times(5/8)^{3}}{12}=0.02035in^{4}/in $$

<!-- batch 30 | page 26 -->

Step 9.Calculate the equivalent elastic stiffness,$K_{E}$ 

$$K_{E}=\frac{384EI}{5bL^{4}}=\frac{384\times29\times10^{6}\times0.02035}{5\times1\times36^{4}}=27.0\mathrm{psi/in}$$

Step 10.Determine the equivalent elastic deflection,$X_{E}$ 

$$X_{F}=r_{u}/K_{F}=25.7/27.0=0.95\ inch $$

Step 11.Calculate the effective mass of element.

$$\begin{array}{r l r}{\mathsf{a.}}&{}&{K_{L M}(\mathsf{a v e r a g e\;e l a s t i c\;a n d\;p l a s t i c})}\\ &{}&{=(0.78+0.66)/2=0.72}\end{array}$$

b.Unit mass of element, m 

$$m=\frac{w}{g}=\frac{5/8\times1\times1\times490\times10^{6}}{1728\times32.2\times12}=458.0\mathrm{~ps}i-\mathrm{ms}^{2}/\mathrm{in}$$

C.Effective unit of mass of element,$m_{e}$ 

$$m_{e}=K_{LM}m=0.72\times458.0$$

Step 12.Calculate the natural period of vibration,$T_{N}$ 

$$T_{N}=2\pi\left(330/27.0\right)^{1/2}=22\mathrm{m s}$$

Step 13.Determine the door response.

Peak overpressure $P=14.8\mathrm{psi}$ 

Peak resistance $r_{u}=25.7\mathrm{p s i}$ 

Duration 

$$T=13.0ms $$

Natural period of vibration $T_{N}=22~\mathrm{m s}$ 

$$P/r_{u}=14.8/25.7=0.58\quad\mathrm{~}\quad T/T_{N}=13.0/22.0=0.59$$

From Figure 3-64a (+) of Chapter 3,

$$X_{m}/X_{F}<1$$

Since the response is elastic, determine the DLF from Figure 3-49$(\pm)$ of Chapter 3.



$$\mathrm{DLF}=1.3\mathrm{for}T/T_N=0.59$$

<!-- batch 30 | page 27 -->

Step 14.Determine the support rotation.

$$X_{m}=\frac{1.3\times14.8\times0.95}{25.7}=0.713\text{inch}$$

$$\tan\Theta=X_{m}/(L/2)=0.713/(36/2)=0.0396$$

$$\Theta=2.27^{\circ}>2^{\circ}\quad N.G.$$

Step 15.Evaluate the selection of the dynamic increase factor.

Since this is an elastic response, use Figure 3-49 (b) (+) of Chapter 3 to determine $t_{m}$ . For $T/T_{N}=0.59,\;t_{m}/T=0.7\;and\;t_{m}=9.1\;ms$ i. The strain rate is:

$$\dot{\varepsilon}=\dot{t}_{d s}/\dot{E}_{s}\dot{t}_{E}$$

(Equation 5-1)

Since the response is elastic,

$$f_{ds}=51.1\times\frac{X_m}{X_E}=51.1\times\frac{0.713}{0.95}=38.4\mathrm{ksi}$$

and $t_{E}=t_{m}=0.0091\mathrm{s e c}$ .Hence,

$$\dot{\varepsilon}=\frac{38.4}{29.6\times10^{3}\times0.0091}=0.142\mathrm{~in}/\mathrm{~se}/\mathrm{~se}$$

Using Figure $5-2(+),DIF=1.31$ . The preliminary selection of $DIF=1.29$ is acceptable.



Since the rotation criterion is not satisfied, change the thickness of the plate and repeat the procedure. Repeating these calculations, it can be shown that a 3/4-inch plate satisfies the requirements.

Step 16.Design of the supporting flexural element.

Assume an angle $\mathsf{L4\times3\times1/2}$ and attached to the plate as shown in Figure 5A-8 (+).



Determine the effective width of plate which acts in conjunction with the angle 



$$b_{f}/2t_{f}<8.5$$

where $b_{f}/2$ is the half width of the outstanding flange or overhang and tf is the thickness of the plate.



With $t_{f}=3/4\ inch,\ b_{f}/2<8.5\times3/4,\ i.e.,\ 6.38\ inches$ 

Use overhang of 6 inches.

$$\bar{y}=\frac{\left(8\times3/4\times3/8\right)+\left(4+3/4-1.33\right)\times3.25}{\left(8\times3/4\right)+3.25}=1.445\ inche $$

Let $y_{p}$ be the distance to the N.A. of the combined section for full plasticity 

$$\begin{aligned}&y_{p}=\frac{1}{8\times2}\left[\left(8\times3/4\right)+3.25\right]=0.578\text{inch}\\ &\quad I=\frac{8\times\left(3/4\right)^{3}}{12}+8\times3/4\times\left(1.45-3/8\right)^{2}\\ &\quad+5.05+3.25\times\left(4.75-1.33-1.45\right)^{2}=24.88\text{in}^{4}\\ &\text{Hence,}S_{min}=24.881/(4.75-1.445)=7.54\text{in}^{3}\\ &\quad Z=8\left(0.578\right)^{2}/2+8\left(0.75-0.578\right)^{2}/2\\ &\quad+3.25\left(4.75-1.33-0.578\right)=10.69\text{in}^{3}\\ \end{aligned}$$

C.Structural configuration (see Figure 5A-9 (+))

Yield strength,$f_{v}=50\mathrm{k s i}$ 

Dynamic increase factor,$DIF=1.24$ (preliminary, Table 5-2 (+))

Average strength increase factor,$S I F=1.1$ (Section 5-12.1)

Hence, the dynamic design stress,

$$f_{ds}=1.1\times1.24\times50=68.2\mathrm{ksi}$$

Note:

It is assumed, for the limited design rotation of $3^{\circ}$ ,that $\mu<10$ ,and,therefore, that Equation 5-3 does not govern.



The dynamic design stress in shear 

$$f_{dv}=0.55f_{ds}=37.5\mathrm{ksi}$$

Step 2.Assume a plate thickness of 2 inches 

Step 3.Determine the elastic and plastic section moduli (per unit width)

$$\begin{aligned}&S=\frac{bd^{2}}{6}=\frac{1\times2^{2}}{6}=0.667\ln^{3}/\ln^{3}\\ &Z=\frac{bd^{2}}{4}=\frac{1\times2^{2}}{4}=1.0\ln^{3}/\ln\\ \end{aligned}$$

Step 4.Calculate the design plastic moment,$M_{P}$ 

$$M_{p}=f_{ds}(S+Z)/2=68.2(0.667+1.0)/2=56.8\mathrm{in}-\mathrm{k/in}$$

Step 5.Calculate the dynamic ultimate shear capacity,$V_{p},$ ,for a 1-inch width.

$$V_{p}=f_{dv}A_{w}=37.5\times2=75.0\ kips/in $$

Step 6.Evaluate the support shear and check the plate shear capacity.

Assume $\mathsf{D L F}=1.0$ 

For simplicity, assume the plate is a one-way member, hence:

$$V=DLF\times P\times L/2=1.0\times1100\times48/2=26.400\;lb/in $$

$=26.4$ kips/in 

<!-- batch 30 | page 34 -->

$$V/V_{p}=26.4/75.=0.352<0.67$$

No reduction in equivalent plastic moment is necessary.

Calculate the ultimate unit resistance,$r_{u}$ ,

For a plate, simply-supported on four sides (direct load)

$$r_{u}=5M_{p}/X^{2}$$

where $M_{HP}=M_{p}and M_{HN}=0and$ 

$$\frac{X}{L}=0.35\mathrm{for}\frac{L}{H}=1.75$$

thus,$X=0.35\times12\times7=29.4$ 

$$r_{u}=5\times56.8/(29.4)^{2}=329\mathrm{psi}$$

Step 8.Compute the moment of inertia, 1, for a 1-inch width 

$$I=\frac{bd^{3}}{12}=\frac{1\times2^{3}}{12}=0.667\ln^{4}/\ln $$

Step 9.Calculate the equivalent elastic stiffness,$K_{E}$ 

$$K_{E}=r/\mathbf{x}=D/\gamma H^{4}$$

where 

$$\gamma=0.0083\qquad(\mathrm{for}\;H/L=0.57)$$

$$D=E I/[b(1-v^{2})]$$

$$D=29.6\times10^{6}\times0.667\left[\left[1\left(1-0.3^{2}\right)\right]=2.17\times10^{7}\right]$$

$$K_{E}=2.17\times10^{7}/0.0083\times48^{4}=492\mathrm{~ps}/\mathrm{in}$$

Step 10.Determine the equivalent elastic deflection,$X_{E}$ 

$$X_{E}=r_{u}/K_{E}=329/492=0.669\ in $$

Step 11.Calculate the effective mass of the element 

$$\begin{aligned}&a.\quad&K_{LM}(average elastic and plastic)\\&\quad&=(0.78+0.66)/2=0.72\\ \end{aligned}$$

b.Unit mass of element 

$$m=\frac{w}{g}=\frac{2\times1\times1\times490\times10^{6}}{1.728\times32.2\times12}=1.468\frac{\mathrm{psi}-\mathrm{ms}^{2}}{\mathrm{in}}$$

<!-- batch 30 | page 35 -->

C.Effective unit mass of element,$m_{e}$ 

$$m_{e}=K_{LM}\times m=0.72\times1.468=1.057\frac{\mathrm{psi}-\mathrm{ms}^{2}}{\mathrm{in}}$$

Step 12.Calculate the natural period of vibration,$T_{N}$ 

$$T_{N}=2\pi\left(1,057/492\right)^{1/2}=9.2\mathrm{m s}$$

Step 13.Determine the door plate response for:

$$\begin{aligned}&P/r_{u}=1100/329=3.34\\&T/T_{N}=1.0/9.2=0.109\\&C_{1}=100/1100=0.091\\&C_{2}>1000\\ \end{aligned}$$

(Figure 3-62 (+), Region C)

Using Figure 3-253 (+/+/+),

$$X_{m}/X_{E}=1.5$$

$$X_{m}=1.5\times0.669=1.00in $$

Step 14.Determine the support rotation.

$$\Theta=\tan^{-1}\left(1/24\right)=2.39^{\circ}<3^{\circ}$$

Step 15.Evaluate the selection of the dynamic increase factor.

$$\dot{\varepsilon}=\dot{t}_{d s}/\dot{E}_{s}\dot{t}_{E}$$

$$t_{E}/T=1.8,t_{E}=1.8\mathrm{m s}$$

(Figure 3-253 (+/+/+))

$$\dot{\varepsilon}=68.2/(29.6\times10^{3}\times0.0018)=1.28in/in/sec $$

$$\mathsf{D I F}=1.3$$

(Figure 5-2 (+), average of A36 and A514)

Initial selection of $\mathrm{DIF}=1.24$ is adequate.

Since the support rotation criteria has been satisfied and the preliminary selection of the DIF is acceptable, a 2 inch thick plate is used in design.

# EXAMPLE 5A-8DESIGN AN I-SHAPED BEAM FOR UNSYMMETRICAL  BENDING DUE TO INCLINED PRESSURE-TIME LOADING 

Required:Design a simply-supported l-shaped beam subjected to a pressuretime loading acting at an angle of 10° with respect to the principal vertical plane of the beam. This beam is part of a structure designed to protect personnel.



Step 1.Given:

a.Pressure-time loading 

b.Design criteria: The structure is to be designed for more than one "shot." A maximum end rota $\mathrm{t i o n=1^{\circ}}$ ', is therefore assigned 

C.Structural configuration 

(Figure 5A-10 (+))

d.Steel used: A36

Yield strength,,$f_{v}=36\mathrm{k s i}$ 

Dynamic increase factor,$DIF=1.29$ 

Average yield strength increase factor,$S I F=1.1$ (Section 5-12.1)

Dynamic design strength,

$$f_{ds}=1.1\times1.29\times36=51.1\mathrm{ksi}$$

Dynamic yielding stress in shear,

$$f_{dv}=0.55f_{ds}=0.55\times51.1=28.1\mathrm{ksi}$$

Modulus of elasticity,$E=29,000,000ps$ 

Step 2.Preliminary sizing of the member.

a.Determine equivalent static load.

Select DL.$F=1.2$ 

$$w=1.25\times4.5\times4.5\times144/1.000=3.65\mathrm{k/ft}$$

<!-- batch 30 | page 39 -->

Figure 5A-10 Beam Configuration and Loading, Example 5A-8(a)

$$b=4.5$$

$$\phi=10^{\circ}$$

b.Determine minimum required $M_{P}$ 

$$M_{p}=(wL^{2})/8=(3.65\times19^{2})/8=165\ k-ft $$

C.Selection of a member.

For a load acting in the plane of the web,

$$\begin{aligned}&(S+Z)=2M_{p}/f_{ds}=(2\times165\times12)/51.1\\&(S+Z)=77.5in^{3}\\ \end{aligned}$$

(S + Z) required $1.4\times77.5=109in^3$ 

$$\begin{aligned}&\mathsf{Try}\mathsf{W}14\times38,S_x=54.6\mathsf{in}^3,Z_x=61.5\mathsf{in}^3,\\&(S+Z)=116.1\mathsf{in}^3,I_x=385\mathsf{in}^4\\&I_y=26.7\mathsf{in}^4\\ \end{aligned}$$

Step 3.Check against local buckling.

For $\mathsf{W14}\times\mathsf{38}$ 

$$\begin{aligned}&d/t_{w}=45.5<(412/(36)^{1/2})(1-1.4\times P/P_{y})=68.66\quad O.K.\quad(Equation5-17)\\&b_{f}/2t_{f}=6.6<8.5\quad O.K.\quad(Section5-24)\\ \end{aligned}$$

<!-- batch 30 | page 40 -->

Step 4.

Inclination of elastic and plastic neutral axes with respect to the x-axis.

$$\begin{aligned}&\tan\alpha=(I_x/I_y)\tan\phi=(385/26.7)\tan10^\circ=2.546\quad(\text{Equation5-24})\\&\alpha=68.5^\circ\\ \end{aligned}$$

Calculate the equivalent elastic section modulus.

$$\begin{aligned}&S=(S_x S_y)/(S_y\cos\phi+S_x\sin\phi)\\&S_x=54.6in^3,S_y=7.88in^3,\phi=10^\circ\\&\sin10^\circ=0.174,\cos10^\circ=0.985\\&S=(54.6)(7.88)/(7.88\times0.985+54.7\times0.174)=24.9in^3\\ \end{aligned}$$

Step 5.Calculate the plastic section modulus, Z.

$$\begin{aligned}&Z=A_{c}m_{1}+A_{t}m_{2}\\&A_{c}=A_{t}=A/2=11.2/2=5.6in^{2}\\ \end{aligned}$$

(Equation 5-6)

Step 6.Determine design plastic moment,$M_{P}$ 

$$\begin{aligned}M_{p}&=f_{ds}(S+Z)/2=51.1(24.9+56.5)/2\\&=51.1\times40.7=2.080in-kips\end{aligned}$$

Step 7.Calculate ultimate unit resistance,$r_{u}.$ 

$$r_{u}=8M_{p}/L^{2}=(8)(2,080)(1,000)/(19\times12)^{2}=320\ lb/in $$

Step 8.Compute elastic deflection, δ.

$$\begin{aligned}\delta=&\left[\left(\delta_{x}^{2}+\delta_{y}^{2}\right)\right]^{1/2}\\\delta_{y}=&\frac{5w\cos\phi L^{4}}{384EI_{x}}\\\delta_{x}=&\frac{5w\sin\phi L^{4}}{384EI_{y}}\\w=&\mathrm{e}_{\mathrm{equiv}}\mathrm{int}_{\mathrm{int}}\mathrm{static}\mathrm{load}+\mathrm{head}\mathrm{load}\\=&2.92+\frac{(4.8\times4.5)+38}{1000}\mathrm{kps}/\mathrm{ft}=2.94\mathrm{kps}/\mathrm{ft}\\\delta=&\left[\frac{\left(5w\sin\phi L^{4}\right)^{2}}{384EI_{y}}+\frac{\left(5w\cos\phi L^{4}\right)^{2}}{384EI_{x}}\right]^{1/2}\\=&\frac{5w L^{4}}{38400E}\times\left[0.652^{2}+0.256^{2}\right]^{1/2}=2.08\mathrm{mech}\end{aligned}$$

<!-- batch 30 | page 42 -->

Step 9.Calculate the equivalent elastic unit stiffness,$K_{E}$ 

$$K_{E}=\frac{w}{\delta}=\frac{2.94\times1.000\times1}{12\times2.085}=117.81b/in^{2}$$

(Get w from Step 8)

Step 10.Determine the equivalent elastic deflection,$X_{E}$ 

$$X_{E}=r_{u}/K_{E}=320/117.8=2.72\ inche $$

Step 11.Calculate the effective mass of the element,$m_{e}$ 

a.Load-mass factor,$K_{L M}$ 

KLM (average elastic and plastic)

$$(0.78+0.66)/2=0.72$$

b.Unit mass of element, m 

$$m=\frac{w}{g}=\frac{\left[(4.5\times4.8)+38\right]\times10^{6}}{32.2\times12\times12}=1.286\times10^{4}\mathrm{~lb}-\mathrm{ms}^{2}/\mathrm{in}^{2}$$

C.Effective unit mass of element,$\mathsf{m_{e}}$ 

$$m_{e}=K_{LM}m=0.72\times1.286\times10^{4}=0.93\times10^{4}\ lb-ms^{2}/in^{2}$$

Step 12.Calculate the natural period of vibration,$T_{N}$ 

$$T_{N}=2\pi\left[(93\times10^{2})/117.8\right]^{1/2}=55.8~\mathrm{m s}$$

(Section 5-22.2)

Step 13.Determine the beam response.

$$\begin{aligned}&\mathsf{Pexak overpressure}\quad&P&=4.5\times4.5\times12=243\mathsf{Ib/in}\\&\mathsf{Pexak}\mathsf{resistance}\quad&r_u&=320\mathsf{Ib/in}\\&\mathsf{Duration}\quad&T&=20\mathsf{ms}\\ \end{aligned}$$

Natural period of vibration $T_{N}=55.8~\mathrm{m s}$ 

$$P/r_{u}=243/320=0.76一
$$

$$T/T_{N}=20/55.8=0.358$$

From Figure 3-64a (+)

$$X_{m}/X_{E}<1$$

From Figure 3-49 (+), for $T/T_{N}=0.358$ 

DL $F=0.97$ 

$$Hence X_{m}=0.97\times4.5\times4.5\times12/117.8=2.0in $$

<!-- batch 30 | page 43 -->

Find end rotation, Θ.

$$\begin{aligned}&\tan\Theta=X_{m}/(L/2)=2.0/[(19\times12)/2]=0.0175\\ &\quad\Theta=1.0^{\circ}\quad\text{O.K.}\\ \end{aligned}$$

Step 14.Calculate the dynamic ultimate shear capacity,$V_{p},$ and check for adequacy.



$$\begin{aligned}&V_{p}=f_{dv}A_{w}=28.1(14.10-2\times0.515)(0.310)=113.9kips(Equation5-16)\\&V=DLF\times P\times b\times L/2\\&\quad=0.97\times4.5\times4.5\times19\times144/(2\times1,000)\\&\quad=26.9kips<89.2kips<V_{p}\quad O.K.\quad(Table3-9(+))\\ \end{aligned}$$
